```text
1	\section{Per-Generator Equivalence Testing}
2	\label{app:tost}
3	
4	To confirm that the near-stationary classification is not a pooling artifact, we apply two one-sided $t$-tests (TOST) \cite{lakens_equivalence} for each feature within each generator separately (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+, equivalence bound $|d| < 0.20$).
5	We adopt Cohen's conventional small effect size ($d{=}0.20$) \cite{cohen1988statistical} as the smallest effect of practical significance (SESOI): effects below this threshold are unlikely to be practically meaningful for depth discrimination.
6	LOFO analysis independently supports this choice: removing any near-stationary feature changes classification F1 by $\leq$1.5\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:lofo}), linking the statistical threshold to classification irrelevance.
7	Sensitivity analysis at $\varepsilon \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25\}$ confirms the partition is robust to the bound choice (see DDR Validation appendix).
8	Table~\ref{tab:tost-per-gen} reports observed $|d|$; \checkmark{} marks features for which the TOST null hypothesis (non-equivalence) is rejected at $\alpha = 0.05$.
9	
10	Entropy min passes equivalence for all eight generators; entropy max passes for six of eight (exceeding $\varepsilon$ only for Pythia-1B and Pythia-2.8B, where $|d| \approx 0.21$--$0.25$); tail decay passes for four of eight.
11	All drifting and intermediate features fail equivalence uniformly, with $|d|$ ranging from 0.23 (entropy std, Mistral) to 1.40 (entropy mean, Pythia-1B).
12	The partition is robust at the individual-generator level, with entropy min as the most consistently stationary feature.
13	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-scatter} visualizes the DDR partition in ($|\rho|$, $d$) space, showing a clear gap between the three groups.
```

```json
      "content": "\\section{Per-Generator Equivalence Testing}\n\\label{app:tost}\n\nTo confirm that the near-stationary classification is not a pooling artifact, we apply two one-sided $t$-tests (TOST) \\cite{lakens_equivalence} for each feature within each generator separately (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+, equivalence bound $|d| < 0.20$).\nWe adopt Cohen's conventional small effect size ($d{=}0.20$) \\cite{cohen1988statistical} as the smallest effect of practical significance (SESOI): effects below this threshold are unlikely to be practically meaningful for depth discrimination.\nLOFO analysis independently supports this choice: removing any near-stationary feature changes classification F1 by $\\leq$1.5\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:lofo}), linking the statistical threshold to classification irrelevance.\nSensitivity analysis at $\\varepsilon \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25\\}$ confirms the partition is robust to the bound choice (see DDR Validation appendix).\nTable~\\ref{tab:tost-per-gen} reports observed $|d|$; \\checkmark{} marks features for which the TOST null hypothesis (non-equivalence) is rejected at $\\alpha = 0.05$.\n\nEntropy min passes equivalence for all eight generators; entropy max passes for six of eight (exceeding $\\varepsilon$ only for Pythia-1B and Pythia-2.8B, where $|d| \\approx 0.21$--$0.25$); tail decay passes for four of eight.\nAll drifting and intermediate features fail equivalence uniformly, with $|d|$ ranging from 0.23 (entropy std, Mistral) to 1.40 (entropy mean, Pythia-1B).\nThe partition is robust at the individual-generator level, with entropy min as the most consistently stationary feature.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:ddr-scatter} visualizes the DDR partition in ($|\\rho|$, $d$) space, showing a clear gap between the three groups.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\footnotesize\n\\caption{Per-generator TOST equivalence ($\\varepsilon{=}|d|{<}0.20$, gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+). ### Tool result
```

_source line 65 · 2026-05-19T03:21:07.930Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TQQZnagG2y1NTHqNBfiqGi`</summary>

```text
1	% Appendix — Length Confound Analysis
2	% Data source: 8-generator LOGO corpus (37,058 samples)
3	
4	Surface diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) mechanically depend on text length:
5	shorter texts yield higher type-token ratios by construction.
6	If generation depth systematically shortens text, observed depth trends could be length artifacts rather than genuine distributional shifts.
7	We investigate with three complementary analyses.
8	
9	\paragraph{(a) Length--depth correlation.}
10	Spearman rank correlation between word count and generation depth is weak across all eight generators ($|\rho| < 0.14$); depth does not systematically shorten or lengthen text in our corpus.
11	
12	\paragraph{(b) Length--feature correlations.}
13	While length--depth correlation is weak, individual features show moderate length dependence:
14	tail\_decay ($|\rho| = 0.38$), hapax\_ratio ($|\rho| = 0.30$), and TTR ($|\rho| = 0.29$).
15	Reference-model features show weaker length correlations (entropy\_mean $|\rho| = 0.09$, entropy\_std $|\rho| = 0.18$).
16	This motivates the length-matched evaluation below: if length mediates some depth--feature association, controlling for length will reduce classification performance.
17	
18	\paragraph{(c) Length-matched classification.}
19	We subsample the dataset so that word-count distributions are equalized across depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+), then retrain the binary XGBoost classifier under pooled LOGO (8-fold).
20	Length-matched binary F1\,=\,0.724, down from 0.770 on the unmatched set ($\Delta$\,=\,$-$0.046).
21	To distinguish this from a sample-size effect, we run a random-subsample control at equal $N$ without length matching: F1\,=\,0.767, which confirms that the 0.046 drop reflects genuine length deconfounding rather than reduced data.
22	
23	\paragraph{Conclusion.}
24	Length accounts for approximately 6\% of discriminative performance ($\Delta$\,=\,0.046 out of 0.770).
25	The majority of the depth signal is length-independent: length-matched F1 (0.724) remains far above chance and significantly above all individual detection baselines (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
26	Surface features (TTR, hapax\_ratio) partially co-vary with length, but their depth-discriminative contribution persists after length control, consistent with generation-induced vocabulary compression operating independently of text truncation.
27	
```

```text
1	Table~\ref{tab:confusion} reports the aggregated confusion matrix from the original six-generator subset (Pythia-2.8B, Pythia-1B, OLMo-2-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B) under leave-one-generator-out (LOGO) evaluation, collapsed to three ordinal classes: gen-0 (human), gen-1 (first-generation), and gen-2+ (depth $\geq$ 2).
2	The later-added Llama-3.2-3B and Mistral-7B-v0.3 are evaluated in the full 8-generator LOGO results (Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
3	
4	\begin{table}[h]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
8	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
9	\toprule
10	& \textbf{Pred gen-0} & \textbf{Pred gen-1} & \textbf{Pred gen-2+} \\

12	\textbf{True gen-0} & 5{,}937 (99.4\%) & 35 (0.6\%) & 0 (0.0\%) \\
13	\textbf{True gen-1} & 368 (6.2\%) & 4{,}437 (75.0\%) & 1{,}113 (18.8\%) \\
14	\textbf{True gen-2+} & 464 (3.0\%) & 3{,}646 (23.3\%) & 11{,}529 (73.7\%) \\
15	\bottomrule
16	\end{tabular}}
17	\caption{Aggregated 3-class confusion matrix from 6-generator LOGO evaluation ($N=27{,}529$). Each cell shows count and row-normalized percentage. Gen-0 is near-perfectly identified. Gen-1 errors are asymmetric: misclassification as gen-2+ (18.8\%) is 3$\times$ more frequent than as gen-0 (6.2\%). Gen-1 text already carries detectable recursive distortion.}
18	\label{tab:confusion}
19	\end{table}
20	
21	Figure~\ref{fig:confusion-heatmap} provides a heatmap visualization of the confusion matrix.
22	The row-normalized view highlights near-perfect gen-0 classification (99.4\% recall), gen-1 confusion primarily with gen-2+ (18.8\%) rather than gen-0 (6.2\%), and gen-2+ misclassification predominantly into gen-1 (23.3\%), consistent with DDR predictions.
23	
24	\begin{figure}[ht]
25	\centering
26	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_confusion.pdf}
27	\caption{3-class LOGO confusion matrix ($N$\,=\,27{,}529). (a) Raw counts. (b) Row-normalized recall percentages. Gen-0 achieves near-perfect classification; the primary confusion axis is gen-1 $\leftrightarrow$ gen-2+.}
28	\label{fig:confusion-heatmap}
29	\end{figure}
30
```

```text
1	We complement the permutation importance analysis (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) with a leave-one-feature-out ablation. Each row drops one of the seven features and re-evaluates the full 6-gen LOGO protocol; $\Delta$ is the change relative to the full 7-feature ensemble (F1\,=\,0.789).
2	
3	\begin{table}[h]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
7	\toprule
8	\textbf{Dropped feature} & \textbf{LOGO F1} & $\boldsymbol{\Delta}$ \textbf{vs Full} \\
9	\midrule
10	entropy\_max    & 0.774 & $-$1.50\,pp \\
11	entropy\_mean   & 0.783 & $-$0.63\,pp \\
12	ttr             & 0.784 & $-$0.55\,pp \\
13	hapax\_ratio    & 0.785 & $-$0.42\,pp \\
14	tail\_decay     & 0.786 & $-$0.33\,pp \\
15	entropy\_std    & 0.786 & $-$0.29\,pp \\
16	entropy\_min    & 0.789 & $+$0.01\,pp \\
17	\midrule
18	Full (7 features) & 0.789 & --- \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}
21	\caption{Leave-one-feature-out ablation (6-gen LOGO). Each row drops one feature and re-evaluates with the LOGO protocol. Sorted by impact magnitude.}
22	\label{tab:lofo}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\paragraph{Analysis.}
26	Entropy\_max causes the largest degradation when removed ($-$1.5\,pp), despite ranking only fourth in permutation importance (split frequency 0.063). This indicates that entropy\_max provides unique signal (capturing the most surprising token under the reference model) that is not redundant with other features.
27	Conversely, entropy\_min is fully redundant ($\Delta$\,=\,+0.01\,pp), consistent with its near-zero permutation importance (0.015).
28	Six of seven features contribute positively to the ensemble, which confirms that the 7-feature design is not over-specified.
29	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01H5qvSfcQMY1ck2CGGiFHTB`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Independent-Seed Control}
2	\label{app:seed-control}
3	
4	To rule out topic-level leakage from shared seeds across generators, we construct an independent-seed evaluation where Pythia-2.8B and OLMo-2-1B use completely disjoint Wikipedia seed pools (500 seeds each, zero overlap).
5	Each generator produces depth-0 through depth-4 chains on its own seed pool; features are extracted with the same Pythia-410M reference model.
6	We then train a 7-feature XGBoost classifier on one generator's data and test on the other, in both directions.
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
12	\toprule
13	\textbf{Setting} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} \\
14	\midrule
15	Pythia-2.8B $\rightarrow$ OLMo-2-1B & 0.695 & [0.673, 0.717] \\
16	OLMo-2-1B $\rightarrow$ Pythia-2.8B & 0.666 & [0.643, 0.689] \\
17	\midrule
18	Independent-seed average & 0.680 & --- \\
19	Shared-seed LOGO (reference) & 0.694 & [0.688, 0.700] \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}
22	\caption{Independent-seed cross-generator evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Disjoint seed pools eliminate any possibility of topic-based leakage. The average independent-seed F1 (0.680) is within 1.4\,pp of the shared-seed LOGO baseline (0.694), confirming that the classifier captures generative degradation patterns rather than seed-specific topic content.}
23	\label{tab:seed-control}
24	\end{table}
25	
26	The small gap ($-$1.4\,pp) between independent-seed and shared-seed evaluation falls within bootstrap confidence intervals, and both directions individually exceed 0.66.
27	Combined with the seed-level demeaning control (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}), these results provide converging evidence that depth classification is driven by distributional properties of recursive generation, not topic identity.
28	
```

```text
1	We evaluate finer-grained 5-class depth classification (gen-0 through gen-4) using the same 7-feature ensemble and LOGO protocol described in \S\ref{sec:method}.
2	
3	\begin{table}[h]
4	\centering
5	\small
6	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
7	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
8	\toprule
9	\textbf{Held-out Generator} & \textbf{Macro F1} & \textbf{Weighted F1} & \textbf{Accuracy} \\
10	\midrule
11	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.484 & 0.506 & 0.524 \\
12	Pythia-1B        & 0.543 & 0.568 & 0.575 \\
13	Pythia-2.8B      & 0.523 & 0.540 & 0.560 \\
14	Qwen3-0.6B      & 0.513 & 0.521 & 0.546 \\
15	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.510 & 0.536 & 0.548 \\
16	Qwen3-8B        & 0.434 & 0.455 & 0.493 \\
17	\midrule
18	\textbf{Average} & \textbf{0.501 $\pm$ 0.038} & \textbf{0.521} & \textbf{0.541} \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}}
21	\caption{5-class LOGO evaluation. Each row holds out one generator for testing, training on the remaining five.}
22	\label{tab:5class-logo}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	\begin{table}[h]
26	\centering
27	\small
28	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
29	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
30	\toprule
31	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Gen-0} & \textbf{Gen-1} & \textbf{Gen-2} & \textbf{Gen-3} & \textbf{Gen-4} \\
32	\midrule
33	Avg F1      & 0.921 & 0.634 & 0.268 & 0.259 & 0.423 \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}}
36	\caption{Per-class F1 averaged across all six LOGO folds.}
37	\label{tab:5class-perclass}
38	\end{table}
39	
40	\paragraph{Analysis.}
41	Moving from 3-class (gen-0 / gen-1 / gen-2+) to 5-class classification reduces LOGO macro F1 to approximately 0.50 (six-generator subset; the 3-class baseline for this subset is comparable to the 8-generator result of 0.762 in Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
42	The degradation is concentrated in the middle depths: gen-2 and gen-3 achieve only 0.268 and 0.259 F1, respectively.
43	The aggregated confusion matrix reveals that 36.5\% of true gen-2 samples are misclassified as gen-1, while 37.8\% of true gen-3 samples are predicted as gen-4.
44	Adjacent depths (gen-2$\leftrightarrow$gen-3, gen-3$\leftrightarrow$gen-4) account for the majority of errors, consistent with the intuition that deeper recursive generations converge in surface-level statistical signatures.
45	Gen-0 (human text) remains highly distinguishable (F1 = 0.921), and gen-1 retains moderate separability (F1 = 0.634).
46	Shape features that are near-stationary in the 3-class setting become substantially more important under 5-class evaluation: permutation importance of entropy\_std increases by 93\% and tail\_decay by 400\% relative to 3-class, yet these gains are insufficient to resolve adjacent depths.
47	These results support the 3-class grouping adopted in the main paper: collapsing gen-2+ preserves the most informative boundary (human vs.\ first-generation vs.\ deeper recursion) while avoiding the noise introduced by near-identical adjacent depths.
48	
49	\paragraph{Classifier comparison.}
50	To verify that 5-class degradation reflects feature-level limitations rather than classifier choice, we evaluate four alternatives under the same protocol: HistGradientBoosting (macro F1\,=\,0.501, 95\% bootstrap CI [0.496, 0.506]), random forest (0.476), logistic regression (0.447), and Frank-Hall ordinal reduction (0.451).
51	All classifiers perform comparably to the default XGBoost ensemble (0.501), ruling out classifier capacity as the bottleneck.
52	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary is the primary failure mode across all methods: these two classes are effectively inseparable with the current feature set. This establishes 3-class granularity as the practical ceiling for surface and entropy features.
53	At the 3-class level, ordinal-specific methods (CORAL ordinal regression, Frank-Hall reduction) similarly fail to improve over multiclass XGBoost (\S\ref{sec:results}), which confirms that the bottleneck is feature separability across all granularities, not the modeling of label ordinality.
54	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0178VPN7K1HHYd3tHYJL9Soq`</summary>

```text
1	We assess robustness to post-generation text modification by randomly replacing words at varying rates and re-evaluating the 8-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\ref{tab:human-edit}).
2	Random word replacement represents a worst-case perturbation: real human editing preserves semantic coherence, so practical degradation is expected to be substantially lower than these estimates.
3	
4	\begin{table}[h]
5	\centering
6	\small
7	\caption{Robustness to random word replacement under 8-gen LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Bootstrap 95\% CIs on binary F1 ($n$\,=\,1000).}
8	\label{tab:human-edit}
9	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
10	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
11	\toprule
12	\textbf{Perturbation rate} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-class F1} \\
13	\midrule
14	0\% (baseline) & 0.695 & [0.680, 0.710] & 0.737 \\
15	5\%            & 0.650 & [0.635, 0.666] & 0.641 \\
16	10\%           & 0.614 & [0.599, 0.629] & 0.492 \\
17	20\%           & 0.591 & [0.576, 0.605] & 0.367 \\
18	\bottomrule
19	\end{tabular}}
20	\end{table}
21	
22	Binary depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) degrades sublinearly: the first 5\% replacement causes the largest drop ($-$4.6\,pp), with diminishing marginal impact thereafter ($-$3.6\,pp for 5\%$\to$10\%, $-$2.3\,pp for 10\%$\to$20\%).
23	At 10\% replacement, binary F1 remains 0.614, well above the 0.50 random baseline, which indicates that depth signal survives moderate perturbation.
24	
25	Three-class classification is more sensitive (0.737$\to$0.367 at 20\%), because distinguishing gen-0 from gen-1 relies heavily on lexical diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) that are directly disrupted by word replacement.
26	The binary metric, which excludes gen-0, is inherently more robust since it depends primarily on reference-model entropy features distributed across many tokens.
27	
28	\paragraph{Feature subset degradation.}
29	Table~\ref{tab:feature-degrade} compares perturbation robustness of the full 7-feature ensemble against an entropy-only subset (5 reference-model features).
30	The higher unperturbed binary F1 of the entropy-only subset (0.833 vs.\ 0.695) reflects the strong discriminability of reference-model features for the coarse binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+); however, lexical features are essential for the finer-grained ordinal task (3-class F1: 0.737 vs.\ 0.688) and provide greater robustness under perturbation.
31	At 20\% replacement, the 7-feature ensemble surpasses entropy-only on binary F1 (0.591 vs.\ 0.569), despite starting from a lower baseline.
32	Random word replacement drastically alters per-token context and makes entropy features (computed over the full token sequence) extremely sensitive to noise.
33	Lexical features (TTR, hapax ratio), as document-level summary statistics, are more stable under per-word perturbation and act as stabilizers in the full ensemble.
34	The crossover confirms that degradation in the full feature set reflects genuine signal disruption rather than a lexical-feature artifact.
35	
36	\begin{table}[h]
37	\centering
38	\small
39	\caption{Feature subset degradation under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO). Entropy-only: 5 reference-model features; 7-feature: full ensemble including lexical features.}
40	\label{tab:feature-degrade}
41	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
42	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
43	\toprule
44	\textbf{Perturb.} & \textbf{Ent-only Bin} & \textbf{7-feat Bin} & \textbf{Ent-only 3-cl} & \textbf{7-feat 3-cl} \\
45	\midrule
46	0\%  & 0.833 & 0.695 & 0.688 & 0.737 \\
47	5\%  & 0.738 & 0.650 & 0.539 & 0.641 \\
48	10\% & 0.667 & 0.614 & 0.427 & 0.492 \\
49	20\% & 0.569 & 0.591 & 0.314 & 0.367 \\
50	\bottomrule
51	\end{tabular}}
52	\end{table}
53	
54	\paragraph{Baseline comparison.}
55	Table~\ref{tab:baseline-robustness} compares robustness across detection methods under identical perturbation conditions (8-gen LOGO, binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
56	
57	\begin{table}[h]
58	\centering
59	\small
60	\caption{Baseline robustness under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO, binary F1). Bold indicates highest F1 at each perturbation rate.}
61	\label{tab:baseline-robustness}
62	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
63	\toprule
64	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{0\%} & \textbf{5\%} & \textbf{10\%} & \textbf{20\%} \\
65	\midrule
66	7-feature ensemble & \textbf{0.695} & 0.650 & 0.614 & 0.591 \\
67	LD-Score           & 0.677 & \textbf{0.673} & \textbf{0.677} & \textbf{0.671} \\
68	Avg-LogProb        & 0.682 & 0.419 & 0.335 & 0.375 \\
69	FastDetectGPT      & 0.571 & 0.437 & 0.468 & 0.514 \\
70	Binoculars\textsuperscript{\dag}         & 0.500 & 0.461 & 0.446 & 0.425 \\
71	\bottomrule
72	\end{tabular}
73	
74	\vspace{2pt}
75	{\footnotesize \textsuperscript{\dag}Binoculars operates at chance level on this binary task; its degradation figures are not meaningful.}
76	\end{table}
77	
78	The 7-feature ensemble achieves the highest unperturbed binary F1 (0.695), but LD-Score surpasses it under perturbation due to near-zero degradation ($-$0.6\,pp across the full 0\%$\to$20\% range).
79	FastDetectGPT and Avg-LogProb exhibit non-monotonic degradation at higher perturbation rates, likely because severe perturbation introduces distributional artifacts that these token-level methods detect as anomalous.
80	Avg-LogProb is the most fragile overall ($-$34.7\,pp at 10\%), while FastDetectGPT drops sharply at 5\% ($-$13.4\,pp) before partially recovering.
81	LD-Score is nearly invariant to perturbation, consistent with its reliance on corpus-level distributional divergence rather than per-token likelihoods.
82	The 7-feature ensemble offers the highest starting performance among single-reference methods, with sublinear degradation ($-$10.4\,pp at 20\%).
83	
84	\paragraph{LLM-simulated sentence regeneration.}
85	To test robustness under semantically coherent editing, we use Qwen3-0.6B-Base to randomly regenerate 1--3 sentences per passage (avg 1.69 sentences, ${\sim}$50\% edit distance) and re-evaluate the 8-gen LOGO pipeline.
86	Binary F1 drops from 0.692 to 0.647 ($-$4.5\,pp, 6.5\% relative); 3-class F1 drops from 0.739 to 0.667 ($-$7.1\,pp, 9.7\% relative).
87	The binary degradation ($-$4.5\,pp) is nearly identical to that caused by 5\% random word replacement ($-$4.6\,pp), despite the LLM rephrase altering ${\sim}$50\% of characters.
88	This asymmetry arises because LLM-generated replacements preserve the underlying statistical signature of the generation process (entropy dynamics, depth-accumulated distributional drift), whereas random word substitution directly destroys local statistical signals.
89	Depth profiling thus captures deep generative structure that survives semantic-preserving edits.
90	
91	\paragraph{Seed complexity.}
92	Performance is stable across seed text complexity: stratifying seeds by vocabulary richness (TTR terciles) yields maximum F1 variation of 1.1\,pp across groups.
93	
```

```json
      "content": "We assess robustness to post-generation text modification by randomly replacing words at varying rates and re-evaluating the 8-generator LOGO pipeline (Table~\\ref{tab:human-edit}).\nRandom word replacement represents a worst-case perturbation: real human editing preserves semantic coherence, so practical degradation is expected to be substantially lower than these estimates.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Robustness to random word replacement under 8-gen LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs on binary F1 ($n$\\,=\\,1000).}\n\\label{tab:human-edit}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Perturbation rate} & \\textbf{Binary F1} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} & \\textbf{3-class F1} \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\% (baseline) & 0.695 & [0.680, 0.710] & 0.737 \\\\\n5\\%            & 0.650 & [0.635, 0.666] & 0.641 \\\\\n10\\%           & 0.614 & [0.599, 0.629] & 0.492 \\\\\n20\\%           & 0.591 & [0.576, 0.605] & 0.367 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nBinary depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+) degrades sublinearly: the first 5\\% replacement causes the largest drop ($-$4.6\\,pp), with diminishing marginal impact thereafter ($-$3.6\\,pp for 5\\%$\\to$10\\%, $-$2.3\\,pp for 10\\%$\\to$20\\%).\nAt 10\\% replacement, binary F1 remains 0.614, well above the 0.50 random baseline, which indicates that depth signal survives moderate perturbation.\n\nThree-class classification is more sensitive (0.737$\\to$0.367 at 20\\%), because distinguishing gen-0 from gen-1 relies heavily on lexical diversity features (TTR, hapax ratio) that are directly disrupted by word replacement.\nThe binary metric, which excludes gen-0, is inherently more robust since it depends primarily on reference-model entropy features distributed across many tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Feature subset degradation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:feature-degrade} compares perturbation robustness of the full 7-feature ensemble against an entropy-only subset (5 reference-model features).\nThe higher unperturbed binary F1 of the entropy-only subset (0.833 vs.\\ 0.695) reflects the strong discriminability of reference-model features for the coarse binary task (gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+); however, lexical features are essential for the finer-grained ordinal task (3-class F1: 0.737 vs.\\ 0.688) and provide greater robustness under perturbation.\nAt 20\\% replacement, the 7-feature ensemble surpasses entropy-only on binary F1 (0.591 vs.\\ 0.569), despite starting from a lower baseline.\nRandom word replacement drastically alters per-token context and makes entropy features (computed over the full token sequence) extremely sensitive to noise.\nLexical features (TTR, hapax ratio), as document-level summary statistics, are more stable under per-word perturbation and act as stabilizers in the full ensemble.\nThe crossover confirms that degradation in the full feature set reflects genuine signal disruption rather than a lexical-feature artifact.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Feature subset degradation under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO). Entropy-only: 5 reference-model features; 7-feature: full ensemble including lexical features.}\n\\label{tab:feature-degrade}\n\\resizebox{\\linewidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Perturb.} & \\textbf{Ent-only Bin} & \\textbf{7-feat Bin} & \\textbf{Ent-only 3-cl} & \\textbf{7-feat 3-cl} \\\\\n\\midrule\n0\\%  & 0.833 & 0.695 & 0.688 & 0.737 \\\\\n5\\%  & 0.738 & 0.650 & 0.539 & 0.641 \\\\\n10\\% & 0.667 & 0.614 & 0.427 & 0.492 \\\\\n20\\% & 0.569 & 0.591 & 0.314 & 0.367 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Baseline comparison.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baseline-robustness} compares robustness across detection methods under identical perturbation conditions (8-gen LOGO, binary: gen-1 vs.\\ gen-2+).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Baseline robustness under random word replacement (8-gen LOGO, binary F1). Bold indicates highest F1 at each perturbation rate.}\n\\label{tab:baseline-robustness}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{0\\%} & \\textbf{5\\%} & \\textbf{10\\%} & \\textbf{20\\%} \\\\\n\\midrule\n7-feature ensemble & \\textbf{0.695} & 0.650 & 0.614 & 0.591 \\\\\nLD-Score           & 0.677 & \\textbf{0.673} & \\textbf{0.677} & \\textbf{0.671} \\\\\nAvg-LogProb        & 0.682 & 0.419 & 0.335 & 0.375 \\\\\nFastDetectGPT      & 0.571 & 0.437 & 0.468 & 0.514 \\\\\nBinoculars\\textsuperscript{\\dag}         & 0.500 & 0.461 & 0.446 & 0.425 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n{\\footnotesize \\textsuperscript{\\dag}Binoculars operates at chance level on this binary task; its degradation figures are not meaningful.}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe 7-feature ensemble achieves the highest unperturbed binary F1 (0.695), but LD-Score surpasses it under perturbation due to near-zero degradation ($-$0.6\\,pp across the full 0\\%$\\to$20\\% range).\nFastDetectGPT and Avg-LogProb exhibit non-monotonic degradation at higher perturbation rates, likely because severe perturbation introduces distributional artifacts that these token-level methods detect as anomalous.\nAvg-LogProb is the most fragile overall ($-$34.7\\,pp at 10\\%), while FastDetectGPT drops sharply at 5\\% ($-$13.4\\,pp) before partially recovering.\nLD-Score is nearly invariant to perturbation, consistent with its reliance on corpus-level distributional divergence rather than per-token likelihoods.\nThe 7-feature ensemble offers the highest starting performance among single-reference methods, with sublinear degradation ($-$10.4\\,pp at 20\\%).\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-simulated sentence regeneration.}\nTo test robustness under semantically coherent editing, we use Qwen3-0.6B-Base to randomly regenerate 1--3 sentences per passage (avg 1.69 sentences, ${\\sim}$50\\% edit distance) and re-evaluate the 8-gen LOGO pipeline.\nBinary F1 drops from 0.692 to 0.647 ($-$4.5\\,pp, 6.5\\% relative); 3-class F1 drops from 0.739 to 0.667 ($-$7.1\\,pp, 9.7\\% relative).\nThe binary degradation ($-$4.5\\,pp) is nearly identical to that caused by 5\\% random word replacement ($-$4.6\\,pp), despite the LLM rephrase altering ${\\sim}$50\\% of characters.\nThis asymmetry arises because LLM-generated replacements preserve the underlying statistical signature of the generation process (entropy dynamics, depth-accumulated distributional drift), whereas random word substitution directly destroys local statistical signals.\nDepth profiling thus captures deep generative structure that survives semantic-preserving edits.\n\n\\paragraph{Seed complexity.}\nPerformance is stable across seed text complexity: stratifying seeds by vocabulary richness (TTR terciles) yields maximum F1 variation of 1.1\\,pp across groups.\n",

      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 93
    }
```

```text
1	We evaluate depth profiling under three prompt types using Pythia-2.8B: \emph{continuation} (``Continue the following text''), \emph{elaboration} (``Elaborate on the following text''), and \emph{inspired rewriting} (``Write a new passage inspired by the following text''). Each uses 995 seeds $\times$ 5 depths.
2	
3	\paragraph{Within-prompt-type classification.}
4	5-fold CV macro-F1: continuation 0.78, rewriting 0.74, elaboration 0.73. All three prompt types exhibit the same differential depth response pattern: drifting features (hapax ratio, TTR, entropy mean) show consistent monotonic trends, while near-stationary features (entropy max, tail decay) change by less than 5\%.
5	
6	\paragraph{Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO).}
7	Leave-one-prompt-type-out evaluation yields average F1\,=\,0.753, which confirms that depth signal transfers across prompt types. Feature decay rates differ: continuation shows the strongest drift (16.9\% per depth step), followed by rewriting (6.1\%) and elaboration (1.4\%), consistent with continuation chains applying less semantic constraint on the output.
8	
```

```text
1	\subsection*{Instruction-Tuned Model Results}
2	
3	Table~\ref{tab:instruct} summarizes depth classification under instruction tuning for three model families.
4	All results use within-model 5-fold CV on single-generator chains; LOGO evaluation is available for two models but uses earlier generator subsets (5--6 generators), so cross-model numbers are not directly comparable across rows.
5	
6	\begin{table}[h]
7	\centering
8	\small
9	\caption{Depth classification: base vs.\ instruction-tuned models (within-model 5-fold CV). $\rho$: Spearman correlation of entropy mean with depth. Rev.: number of features (out of 7) whose $\rho$ reverses sign under instruction tuning.}
10	\label{tab:instruct}
11	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
12	\begin{tabular}{lcc cc cc c}
13	\toprule
14	& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{3-class F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Binary F1}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$\boldsymbol{\rho}$\textbf{(ent.\ mean)}} & \\
15	\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \cmidrule(lr){4-5} \cmidrule(lr){6-7}
16	\textbf{Model} & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & Base & Instruct & \textbf{Rev.} \\
17	\midrule
18	OLMo-2-1B       & 0.754 & 0.575 & 0.845 & 0.813 & $-$0.64 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
19	Qwen3-1.7B      & 0.769 & 0.732 & 0.861 & 0.875 & $-$0.60 & $+$0.48 & 6/7 \\
20	Llama-3.2-3B    & 0.742 & 0.544 & 0.849 & 0.838 & $-$0.63 & $+$0.23 & 3/7 \\
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	Instruction tuning degrades 3-class F1 by 3.7--19.9\,pp across all three families, while binary F1 (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) changes remain small ($|\Delta| \leq 3.2$\,pp).
26	The asymmetry indicates that instruction tuning primarily disrupts the gen-0/gen-1 boundary (human vs.\ first-generation), consistent with RLHF shifting the output distribution away from human-like text.
27	Entropy-mean $\rho$ reverses sign for all three models ($-0.60$--$-0.64$ base $\rightarrow$ $+0.23$--$+0.48$ instruct), and 6 of 7 features reverse for OLMo and Qwen vs.\ 3 of 7 for Llama.
28	This pattern is consistent with instruction tuning shifting the generation attractor rather than eliminating iterative dynamics (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
29
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KDVgnZfKjgwDoPUFvcmZDg`</summary>

```text
1	We evaluate whether depth signal generalizes beyond Wikipedia by constructing generation chains from three additional domains: News articles, Academic abstracts, and Code documentation.
2	For each domain, we sample 200 seed texts and generate chains of depth 3 using three generators (Pythia-1B, Qwen3-1.7B, Mistral-7B).
3	The same 7-feature ensemble and LOGO protocol are applied.
4	
5	\noindent\textbf{Important caveat:} Cross-domain generation used $t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}1.0$, which falls outside the method's reliable operating range ($t \leq 0.7$; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}). Domain and temperature effects are therefore confounded in the results below; the cross-domain signal may partly reflect temperature-induced distributional divergence rather than domain generalization alone.
6	
7	\begin{table}[h]
8	\centering
9	\small
10	\caption{Cross-domain depth classification under LOGO evaluation (binary: gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+). Three generators $\times$ 200 seeds per domain. Bootstrap 95\% CIs ($n$\,=\,1000).}
11	\label{tab:domain}
12	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Domain} & \textbf{Binary F1} & \textbf{95\% CI} & \textbf{3-class F1} \\
15	\midrule
16	Code           & 0.754 & [0.737, 0.770] & 0.634 \\
17	News           & 0.746 & [0.726, 0.764] & 0.652 \\
18	Academic       & 0.739 & [0.720, 0.757] & 0.622 \\
19	\bottomrule
20	\end{tabular}
21	\end{table}
22	
23	All three non-Wikipedia domains achieve binary F1 $>$ 0.73 under leave-one-generator-out evaluation, which demonstrates that depth signal is not an artifact of Wikipedia text properties.
24	
25	\paragraph{Domain $\times$ temperature interaction.}
26	Cross-domain generation used $t{=}1.0$, top-$p{=}1.0$ (vs.\ the main experiment's $t{=}0.7$, top-$p{=}0.9$).
27	Supplementary evaluation at $t{=}0.7$ reveals a domain $\times$ temperature interaction: the main Wikipedia experiment favors lower temperature (depth signal degrades monotonically with rising temperature; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), while cross-domain evaluation favors higher temperature ($t{=}1.0$ yields +3 to +11\,pp over $t{=}0.7$; News 0.746 vs.\ 0.712, Academic 0.739 vs.\ 0.628, Code 0.754 vs.\ 0.687).
28	This asymmetry likely reflects reference-model calibration: Pythia-410M, trained predominantly on web text, detects subtle per-step drift in Wikipedia but requires larger distributional divergence (produced by higher temperature) to detect depth signal in out-of-distribution domains.
29	This finding suggests that reference model selection is a key factor for cross-domain deployment of depth profiling.
30	
31	\paragraph{3-class inversion as DDR evidence.}
32	In the cross-domain setting, 3-class F1 (0.62--0.65) falls below binary F1 (0.74--0.75), reversing the pattern observed in the main experiment (3-class 0.762 $>$ binary 0.694).
33	This inversion reflects degraded gen-0 vs.\ gen-1 separation: the Pythia-410M reference model is calibrated to Wikipedia text, so its baseline entropy estimates are less discriminative for out-of-domain human text and compresses the feature distance between gen-0 and gen-1.
34	The binary task (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) is unaffected because both classes are generated under identical domain and decoding conditions; recursive drift is a relative change that does not depend on absolute calibration.
35	This pattern provides additional evidence that DDR captures generation-process invariants rather than domain-specific text properties: depth signal remains robust even when the human-vs-machine boundary becomes less distinct.
36	
```

_source line 88 · 2026-05-19T03:21:21.427Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AdZnjA6zMgo2zhEJCNy1YV`</summary>

```text
1	We analyze the distribution and characteristics of binary classification errors (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+) across the 8-generator LOGO evaluation ($N$\,=\,29{,}095 samples, overall error rate 28.08\%).
2	
3	\paragraph{Error asymmetry.}
4	Errors are heavily asymmetric: 86.2\% are false negatives (gen-2+ predicted as gen-1) and only 13.8\% are false positives (gen-1 predicted as gen-2+).
5	The classifier systematically under-predicts depth, consistent with the DDR pattern: shallow gen-2+ samples that have not yet accumulated sufficient distributional drift are indistinguishable from gen-1.
6	
7	\paragraph{Per-depth error rates.}
8	Error rates decrease monotonically with depth: depth-2 samples are the primary failure point (48.42\% error rate), followed by depth-3 (29.17\%) and depth-4 (20.32\%).
9	Depth-1 false positive rate is 7.11\%.
10	The monotonic decrease confirms that deeper generations accumulate progressively stronger depth signatures; the gen-1$\to$gen-2 transition is therefore the hardest to detect.
11	
12	\paragraph{Per-generator variation.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:error-by-gen} shows error rates vary substantially across generators, correlating with model scale: larger models (Mistral-7B, Qwen3-8B) produce text closer to gen-1 characteristics, which makes depth discrimination harder.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Binary classification error rates by generator (8-gen LOGO). Sorted by error rate descending.}
19	\label{tab:error-by-gen}
20	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
21	\toprule
22	\textbf{Generator} & \textbf{Family} & \textbf{Samples} & \textbf{Errors} & \textbf{Error rate} \\
23	\midrule
24	Mistral-7B-v0.3 & Mistral & 3{,}853 & 1{,}734 & 45.00\% \\
25	Qwen3-8B        & Qwen    & 3{,}502 & 1{,}197 & 34.18\% \\
26	Llama-3.2-3B    & Llama   & 3{,}685 & 1{,}112 & 30.18\% \\
27	OLMo-2-1B      & OLMo    & 3{,}718 & 1{,}054 & 28.35\% \\
28	Pythia-2.8B     & Pythia  & 3{,}614 &   866 & 23.96\% \\
29	Qwen3-1.7B     & Qwen    & 3{,}621 &   814 & 22.48\% \\
30	Qwen3-0.6B     & Qwen    & 3{,}542 &   745 & 21.03\% \\
31	Pythia-1B       & Pythia  & 3{,}560 &   648 & 18.20\% \\
32	\bottomrule
33	\end{tabular}
34	\end{table}
35	
36	\paragraph{Feature-level explanation.}
37	Mann-Whitney U tests comparing correctly vs.\ incorrectly classified samples reveal that drifting features show significant distributional differences between correct and incorrect predictions: entropy\_mean ($p < 10^{-300}$), TTR ($p < 10^{-300}$), and hapax\_ratio ($p < 10^{-300}$).
38	In contrast, shape features show no discriminating power: entropy\_max ($p = 0.584$) and tail\_decay ($p = 0.653$).
39	Misclassified gen-2+ samples have systematically higher TTR (0.464 vs.\ 0.302 for correct) and entropy\_mean (2.26 vs.\ 1.57); these samples retain gen-1-like lexical diversity and have not undergone the distributional convergence characteristic of deeper generations.
40	This is consistent with the DDR pattern (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}): errors concentrate where depth-accumulated drift is insufficient, and shape features (which are near-stationary by design) provide no additional discriminative signal for error correction.
41	
```

```text
1	\section*{Ethics Statement}
2	\label{sec:ethics}
3	
4	All texts analyzed in this work are LLM-generated from publicly available Wikipedia seeds; no personal or sensitive data is involved.
5	Our method estimates the generation depth of a text passage (how many recursive generation steps it has undergone), not the identity of its author.
6	Individual authorship attribution is outside both the scope and the capability of this approach; privacy implications are minimal.
7	We acknowledge that depth estimation tools could in principle be used to selectively suppress machine-generated text in contexts where such content is legitimate (e.g., accessibility tools, assistive writing).
8	We encourage deployment in data governance pipelines where provenance transparency, rather than content removal, is the objective.
9	All code, data, and trained models will be released upon publication.
10	
11	\paragraph{Licenses.}
12	All generator models are publicly available under permissive licenses (Apache 2.0 for Pythia, OLMo, and Mistral; Qwen License for Qwen3; Llama Community License for Llama 3.2).
13	Wikipedia seed passages are distributed under CC BY-SA 3.0.
14	
15	\paragraph{Compute budget.}
16	All experiments were conducted on 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX 5090 GPUs (32\,GB each), totaling approximately 62 GPU-hours.
17	
18	\paragraph{Software.}
19	Experiments used Python 3.12.3, PyTorch 2.10.0 (CUDA 12.8), Transformers 4.57.6, XGBoost 3.2.0, scikit-learn 1.8.0, NumPy 2.2.6, pandas 3.0.2, and SciPy 1.17.1.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TJ3TBbibZz6oJbMLBqM1eX`</summary>

```text
1	Figure~\ref{fig:cross-temp} shows cross-temperature transfer results for the 6-generator LOGO pipeline.
2	We evaluate four train$\rightarrow$test temperature conditions using Qwen3-1.7B data regenerated at $t{=}1.0$ (remaining five generators retain $t{=}0.7$).\footnote{The $t{=}1.0$ data uses top-$p{=}0.95$ (vs.\ $p{=}0.9$ for other configurations); this difference may contribute to the observed degradation beyond the temperature effect alone.}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[ht]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_cross_temp.pdf}
7	\caption{Cross-temperature transfer evaluation (6-gen LOGO). (a) Overall F1 by train$\rightarrow$test temperature condition. Matched conditions (0.7$\rightarrow$0.7, 1.0$\rightarrow$1.0) outperform cross-temperature transfer, with 0.7$\rightarrow$1.0 showing the largest degradation ($-6.6$\,pp in 3-class F1). (b) Per-generator 3-class F1 heatmap: Qwen3-1.7B shows dramatic degradation under cross-temperature conditions (0.418) while other generators remain stable. The temperature mismatch disproportionately affects the generator whose data was regenerated.}
8	\label{fig:cross-temp}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	Key observations from the cross-temperature analysis:
12	
13	\begin{enumerate}[nosep]
14	\item \textbf{Matched-temperature robustness}: Both $t{=}0.7$ and $t{=}1.0$ matched conditions yield comparable performance (3-class F1: 0.789 vs.\ 0.726), with the gap attributable to weaker depth signal at high temperature rather than classifier degradation.
15	
16	\item \textbf{Asymmetric transfer}: Training at $t{=}1.0$ and testing at $t{=}0.7$ (F1\,=\,0.791) transfers well, but the reverse (0.7$\rightarrow$1.0, F1\,=\,0.723) degrades. This asymmetry reflects the information structure: features learned from high-variance (high-temperature) data generalize to low-variance settings, but not vice versa.
17	
18	\item \textbf{Generator-specific vulnerability}: Qwen3-1.7B, the only generator with temperature-mismatched test data, drops to F1\,=\,0.418 under cross-temperature conditions while all other generators maintain F1 $>$ 0.76. This confirms that depth features are calibrated to the specific decoding regime.
19	
20	\item \textbf{Binary transfer for the mismatched generator}: For Qwen3-1.7B (the only generator with actual temperature mismatch), binary F1 drops from 0.948 to 0.713 under 0.7$\rightarrow$1.0 transfer but recovers in the reverse direction (1.0$\rightarrow$0.7: 0.948), consistent with the asymmetric pattern in Item 2. The aggregate binary range (0.900--0.958) is inflated by five generators whose data is identical across conditions.
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
```

```text
7	\label{tab:feature-corr}
8	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
9	\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrrr}
10	\toprule
11	 & $\bar{H}$ & $\sigma_H$ & $H_{\min}$ & $H_{\max}$ & $\tau$ & TTR & Hapax \\
12	\midrule
13	$\bar{H}$ & \textemdash & 0.49 & 0.31 & 0.01 & 0.07 & \textbf{0.86} & \textbf{0.81} \\
14	$\sigma_H$ & 0.49 & \textemdash & $-$0.15 & 0.16 & 0.26 & 0.43 & 0.34 \\
15	$H_{\min}$ & 0.31 & $-$0.15 & \textemdash & $-$0.04 & 0.01 & 0.20 & 0.23 \\
16	$H_{\max}$ & 0.01 & 0.16 & $-$0.04 & \textemdash & $-$0.08 & $-$0.13 & $-$0.15 \\
17	$\tau$ & 0.07 & 0.26 & 0.01 & $-$0.08 & \textemdash & 0.13 & 0.14 \\
18	TTR & \textbf{0.86} & 0.43 & 0.20 & $-$0.13 & 0.13 & \textemdash & \textbf{0.97} \\
19	Hapax & \textbf{0.81} & 0.34 & 0.23 & $-$0.15 & 0.14 & \textbf{0.97} & \textemdash \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	TTR and hapax ratio are near-perfectly correlated ($r=0.97$), and both correlate strongly with mean entropy ($r=0.86$ and $0.81$).
25	These three lexical-diversity features form a tightly coupled cluster.
26	In contrast, $H_{\max}$, $\tau$ (tail decay), and $H_{\min}$ show weak pairwise correlations with all other features ($|r| < 0.27$), confirming that they capture independent aspects of the token-probability distribution.
27
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01AfhBNhUdgnsU7ofpfyfjKu`</summary>

```text
1	To assess whether depth signal extends beyond handcrafted features, we fine-tune a pretrained language model end-to-end as a depth classifier using QLoRA \cite{qlora}.
2	
3	\paragraph{Setup.}
4	We fine-tune Qwen3-0.6B-Base with LoRA rank 16, learning rate $2 \times 10^{-4}$, and 3 epochs, framing depth estimation as 5-class sequence classification (gen-0 through gen-4).\footnote{Evaluated on the original 6-generator subset; XGBoost 3-class F1\,=\,0.789. We expect 8-generator training to further improve given additional diversity; the comparison direction is preserved.}
5	For 3-class evaluation, we collapse predictions into gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+ post hoc.
6	All results use the same LOGO protocol as the main experiments.
7	
8	\begin{table}[h]
9	\centering
10	\small
11	\caption{QLoRA end-to-end depth classification vs.\ XGBoost on handcrafted features, under LOGO evaluation.}
12	\label{tab:qlora}
13	\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{5-class F1} & \textbf{3-class F1} & \textbf{Per-class F1 (5-class)} \\
17	\midrule
18	XGBoost (7 features) & 0.501 $\pm$ 0.038 & 0.789 & --- \\
19	QLoRA (Qwen3-0.6B)   & 0.583 $\pm$ 0.010 & 0.905 $\pm$ 0.003 & 0.999\,/\,0.796\,/\,0.413\,/\,0.259\,/\,0.447 \\
20	\bottomrule
21	\end{tabular}}
22	\end{table}
23	
24	\paragraph{Results.}
25	QLoRA achieves 3-class LOGO F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.003), a +11.6\,pp improvement over the XGBoost baseline (0.789).
26	On 5-class evaluation, QLoRA reaches F1\,=\,0.583 ($\pm$0.010) vs.\ 0.501 for XGBoost (+8.2\,pp), with the largest gains at gen-1 (0.796 vs.\ 0.634) and gen-2 (0.413 vs.\ 0.268).
27	The gen-2/gen-3 boundary remains the primary failure mode (gen-3 F1\,=\,0.259), consistent with the feature-level analysis in \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
28	
29	\paragraph{Hyperparameter robustness.}
30	A second run with LoRA rank 32 and seed 123 yields 5-class F1\,=\,0.587 ($\pm$0.005) and 3-class F1\,=\,0.905 ($\pm$0.004), which confirms stability across hyperparameter and seed variation ($\Delta < 1$\,pp on both metrics).
31	
32	\paragraph{Implications.}
33	The substantial 3-class improvement demonstrates that raw token embeddings contain depth signal beyond what summary statistics capture; the seven handcrafted features are a strong but lossy compression of the full distributional information.
34	End-to-end fine-tuning exploits token-level patterns (e.g., positional regularities, n-gram repetitions) that aggregate features cannot represent.
35	This confirms that the depth signal documented in our main analysis is real and exploitable beyond engineered features, while the handcrafted approach retains practical advantages: interpretability, computational efficiency (no GPU inference), and the ability to reveal the differential depth response pattern that motivates the task formulation.
36	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Qualitative Examples}
2	\label{app:qualitative}
3	
4	Table~\ref{tab:qualitative-text} presents an example generation chain from seed 120 (Pythia-2.8B, continuation prompt), illustrating how text evolves across recursive generation steps.
5	Table~\ref{tab:qualitative-features} reports the corresponding 7-feature values. Central-tendency features ($\bar{H}$, TTR, hapax ratio) drift monotonically while shape features ($H_{\max}$, tail decay) remain near-stationary, consistent with the differential depth response pattern.
6	
7	\begin{table}[t]
8	\centering
9	\caption{Example text chain from seed 120 (Pythia-2.8B, continuation prompt). Gen-0 is the original Wikipedia passage; gen-1 and gen-2 are successive LLM continuations. Text is truncated for space.}
10	\label{tab:qualitative-text}
11	\small
12	\begin{tabular}{p{0.93\columnwidth}}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Gen-0 (Wikipedia seed)} \\
15	\midrule
16	Aquarius is an equatorial constellation of the zodiac, between Capricornus and Pisces. Its name is Latin for ``water-carrier'' or ``cup-carrier''. Aquarius is one of the oldest of the recognized constellations along the zodiac (the Sun's apparent path). It was one of the 48 constellations listed by the 2nd century astronomer Ptolemy, and it remains one of the 88 modern constellations. It is found in a region often called the Sea due to its profusion of constellations with watery associations such as Cetus the whale, Pisces the fish, and Eridanus the river. [\ldots] \\
17	\midrule
18	\textbf{Gen-1} \\
```

```text
1	\subsection*{DDR Trend Lines}
2	
3	Figure~\ref{fig:ddr-trends} shows the per-feature depth trend (mean across all samples at each depth) with individual generator traces overlaid.
4	The consistent downward trajectories for drifting features across all generators confirm that DDR is a universal property of recursive generation.
5	Shape features exhibit flat trajectories regardless of generator, with near-zero $R^2$ for the linear fit.
6	
7	\FloatBarrier
8	\begin{figure*}[ht]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_appendix_ddr_trends.pdf}
11	\caption{Per-feature depth trends. Black line: pooled mean ($\pm$1 std shading) across all generators. Colored traces: individual generators. Features are labeled by DDR category (drifting, intermediate, near-stationary). Spearman $\rho$ and linear $R^2$ are annotated per panel.}
12	\label{fig:ddr-trends}
13	\end{figure*}
14	
15	\subsection*{UMAP Embedding}
16	
17	Figure~\ref{fig:umap} shows a 2D UMAP projection of the 7-feature space (7{,}500 stratified samples, StandardScaler normalization, $k$\,=\,30 neighbors, min\_dist\,=\,0.3).
18	Panel (a) reveals a depth gradient from gen-0 through gen-4, with progressive overlap at deeper generations consistent with the convergence that motivates the gen-2+ class collapse.
19	Panel (b) shows the 3-class view: gen-0 forms a separable cluster, while gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap substantially, which explains the classification difficulty at this boundary.
20	
21	\FloatBarrier
22	\begin{figure}[ht]
23	\centering
24	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig_appendix_umap.pdf}
25	\caption{UMAP 2D projection of the 7-feature space ($N$\,=\,7{,}500 stratified sample). (a) Colored by raw generation depth (0--4): a gradient structure emerges, with deeper depths converging. (b) Colored by 3-class label: gen-0 separates clearly; gen-1 and gen-2+ overlap, consistent with the high false-negative rate at the gen-1/gen-2+ boundary.}
26	\label{fig:umap}
27	\end{figure}
```

_source line 154 · 2026-05-19T03:23:58.643Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
I've now read the entire paper thoroughly, including all appendices, and examined the PDF rendering. Let me also verify the references before writing my review.

Let me now compile my review. I have a comprehensive understanding of the paper. Let me organize my findings by the review dimensions.

Key observations:

**Paper overview:**
- The paper introduces "generation depth classification" - classifying text as gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+ rather than binary real-vs-synthetic
- Identifies a "differential depth response" (DDR) with a 3/1/3 tri-partition of features
- Uses 8 base models from 5 families (0.6B-8B params)


- A 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1=0.694 under LOGO evaluation, though the approach is limited to base pretrained models since instruction tuning degrades the signal

**Strengths I can identify:**
1. Well-defined task formulation (generation depth classification)
2. Thorough experimental controls (LOGO, LOFO, seed sharing control, length confound, independent seed)
3. Comprehensive ablation studies
4. Honest about limitations (instruction tuning, scale, temperature)
5. DDR tri-partition is well-documented with multiple convergent metrics
6. QLoRA upper bound confirms depth signal exists beyond handcrafted features

**Weaknesses - Method:**

1. **[Should-Fix] Cohen's d under heteroscedasticity**: The paper acknowledges Levene tests reject equal variance (p < 10^-10) with variance ratios of 1.86-2.21 for drifting features, and they do supplement with Glass's Δ and Cliff's δ. However, the main text still relies on pooled Cohen's d as the primary characterization, presenting "Cohen's d > 0.75" as the drifting threshold without adequately caveating the heteroscedasticity issue upfront—this analysis gets relegated to the appendix.

2. **[Should-Fix] Tri-partition thresholds**: The 3/1/3 partition uses domain-informed criteria (Cohen's d > 0.75, d < 0.10) that are framed as data-driven but are actually somewhat arbitrary choices.  The data-driven clustering approach itself suggests k=2 as optimal (silhouette=0.632), and the 3-way split is explicitly a "domain-informed refinement." While the paper is transparent about this, the key thresholds lack strong justification beyond convention.

3. **[Minor] Entropy terminology**: The paper uses "entropy" as shorthand for per-token NLL with a caveat in Section 3.3, which is acceptable but potentially confusing.

4. **[Must-Fix] Gen-0 seed sharing and 3-class metric inflation**: There's a fundamental design issue where gen-0 texts are shared across generators, meaning when a generator is held out in LOGO, its gen-0 texts remain identical to those in the training generators' chains, which could artificially inflate the 3-class metrics.

The paper acknowledges this and pivots to binary F1 (gen-1 vs gen-2+) as the primary metric, but the 3-class F1 is still prominently reported in Table 1 and mentioned in the abstract (0.762), with the confusion matrix showing a trivially inflated gen-0 recall of 99.4%. The core contribution claims rely on "3-class" evaluation results, yet the only reliable evaluation is actually the binary gen-1 vs gen-2+ distinction, meaning the stated task of 3-class depth classification can't be cleanly evaluated. Looking more closely at Section 4.1, the paper does acknowledge that gen-0 classification is near-perfect due to the shared seeds, with per-class F1 around 0.94.

They've addressed this with seed-level demeaning (F1=0.701) and an independent-seed experiment (F1=0.680), which shows reasonable effort to handle the concern. However, the fundamental problem remains: they claim to evaluate "generation depth classification (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+)" but only reliably measure gen-1 vs gen-2+, since gen-0 is trivially easy due to seed overlap. The independent-seed experiment only uses 2 generators rather than the full LOGO setup, so a proper 3-class evaluation across all generators with truly independent seeds is still missing.

The binary F1 of 0.694 is above chance but modest—with a 28% error rate overall and 45% for Mistral-7B, the practical utility is limited. On the LOGO evaluation front, they acknowledge that holding out individual generators doesn't isolate family effects (Pythia-1B held out but Pythia-2.8B remains), and while LOFO addresses this with F1=0.686, I need to verify what that evaluation actually covers.

Looking at the family composition—5 families total with Pythia having 2 models, Qwen having 3, and others single models—holding out single-model families like Llama or Mistral means training on 7 generators and testing on 1, which seems reasonable. But the paper only reports the average LOFO result without full details. There's also the wrinkle that Pythia-410M is the reference model, so when holding out the entire Pythia family, the reference is still from that family. The cross-reference experiment shows only 0.013 spread though, so this might not be a major concern.

The downstream utility experiment with just 3 seeds is severely underpowered for the TOST test, which the paper itself acknowledges.  The Cohen's d values of 86.0 and 119.7 are unrealistic but stem from the low variance with such a small sample. This is critical for supporting the claim that depth composition doesn't determine training utility, yet the evidence is weak.

The prompt type experiment only uses Pythia-2.8B as the generator, which significantly limits claims about cross-prompt-type transfer. And the cross-domain experiment operates at t=1.0 with top-p=1.0, which falls outside the method's reliable range of t ≤ 0.7—the paper does provide supplementary results at t=0.7, but the primary cross-domain findings are confounded by this temperature choice.

The paper also uses different generator subsets (5, 6, and 8) across experiments, which complicates comparisons between studies and raises questions about selective reporting, though the authors attribute this to chronological expansion.

For the statistical analysis, the bootstrap confidence intervals use percentile bootstrap, which is the simplest approach and can introduce bias, but the paper doesn't clarify this choice. McNemar tests are applied for pairwise comparisons without specifying whether continuity correction is used or which variant is employed.

The TOST equivalence bound of d=0.20 relies on Cohen's conventional small effect size, which feels somewhat arbitrary—the sensitivity analysis across ε ∈ {0.15, 0.20, 0.25} helps, but the main results hinge on this threshold, and notably tail decay only passes 4 out of 8 generators at ε=0.20, which weakens the near-stationary claim. The multiple comparison corrections are applied within families, but the family definitions seem inconsistent—the 21 distributional comparisons and 4 pairwise baseline comparisons receive separate corrections rather than a unified global correction.

On the baseline side, there's a critical issue: the 4-detector ensemble actually achieves binary F1=0.708, slightly outperforming the 7-feature ensemble at 0.705, yet the paper frames this as marginally higher at 2.4× inference cost. This undermines the value proposition of the DDR framework as a classification tool, since off-the-shelf detectors can match depth discrimination performance. The paper's interpretation that "detection scores achieve comparable depth discrimination suggests depth information is implicitly encoded" cuts both ways. Additionally, the paper lacks comparisons with more recent detection methods and simpler baselines like n-gram or perplexity-based approaches beyond the four included.

Looking at missing analyses, the classifier's predicted probabilities aren't evaluated for calibration, which is important given the corpus-level flagging use case mentioned in the discussion. The actual length distributions per depth aren't shown either, despite doing length-matched analysis. More significantly, the instruct model evaluation is too narrow—only 3 models tested with acknowledged degradation, yet instruct models dominate real-world generation, making this a major practical limitation that constrains the paper's impact.

I'm also noting several questions for the authors: the independent-seed experiment only uses 2 generators, so I'd want results across more. The cross-reference experiment uses 5-gen LOGO rather than the full 8-generator setup, so it's unclear if those findings generalize. And there are follow-up questions about the experimental design that need clarification. the paper's practical scope and modest performance gains. The work makes a solid scientific contribution to understanding recursive generation patterns, but the restriction to base models and the modest binary F1 score of 0.694 limit its real-world applicability, especially given that a simpler 4-detector ensemble achieves comparable results to the full 7-feature approach.

Now I'm reconsidering the rating against the EMNLP review scale—the paper is well-written and introduces a novel task formulation with thorough empirical analysis, which pushes it toward a solid Findings-level contribution despite the limitations.
```

```json
{
  "text": "\n# Reviewer 1 审稿意见 — Round 1\n\n**论文**: \"Depth Profiling: Classifying Recursive Generation Depth in Base LLM Text Chains\"\n**Venue**: EMNLP 2026 (ARR Long Paper)\n**审查侧重**: 方法严谨性 + 实验设计 + 统计分析\n\n---\n\n## Summary\n\n本文提出 generation depth classification 任务，将文本分类为 gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+（而非传统的二元 real-vs-synthetic），并在 8 个 base model（5 个 family，0.6B–8B 参数）上识别出 differential depth response (DDR) 三分结构：3 个 drifting 特征（entropy mean, TTR, hapax ratio）、1 个 intermediate 特征（entropy std）、3 个 near-stationary 特征（entropy max, tail decay, entropy min）。7 特征集成在 LOGO 评估下获得 leak-free binary F1 = 0.694。论文诚实地承认了 base model 限制和 instruction tuning 下信号退化的问题。\n\n---\n\n## 总体评分\n\n**Overall**: 3.0 (Findings)\n**一句话总结**: 任务定义新颖、实验控制严谨、DDR 三分结构是有价值的经验性发现，但 base-model-only 限制严重削弱实际意义，primary metric 偏低，且评估设计中 gen-0 seed sharing 使核心三分类任务无法被干净评估。\n\n## 官方维度打分（来自 ACL ARR review form）\n\n| 维度 | 分数 | 理由摘要 |\n|------|------|----------|\n| Soundness | 3/5 | 方法论整体合理，DDR tri-partition 有多指标交叉验证支撑。但 Cohen's d 在异方差条件下的使用、tail_decay 仅 4/8 通过 TOST 等效检验、downstream TOST 仅 n=3 seeds 等问题削弱部分 claim 的统计可靠性 |\n| Excitement | 2.5/5 | 任务定义新颖（depth classification vs binary detection），DDR 分解提供了有价值的分析框架。但仅限 base model 的结论在 instruct model 主导的真实场景中适用性极低，binary F1=0.694 的实用价值有限 |\n| Reproducibility | 4/5 | 实验细节充分（模型、超参数、软件版本、GPU 小时），承诺公开代码和数据。多个 generator subset 虽有解释，但增加了复现复杂度 |\n| Overall Assessment | 3.0 | Findings 水平——有真实的科学贡献（DDR 三分结构），但 base-model 限制、中等性能、gen-0 评估污染共同限制了影响力 |\n| Confidence | 4/5 | 仔细审阅了全文及所有附录，熟悉 AI-generated text detection 和统计检验方法 |\n\n---\n\n## Must-Fix 问题（影响接收决定）\n\n**N1** [critical] Gen-0 seed sharing 使核心三分类评估失效，论文的 framing 与实际可靠评估之间存在落差\n- 具体位置：§3.2, §4.1, Table 1, Abstract\n- 问题：论文将任务定义为 \"generation depth classification (gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+)\"（Abstract line 2, §1 Contribution 1），但由于 gen-0 seeds 在所有 generators 间共享，LOGO 评估中 gen-0 文本在训练集和测试集中完全相同。论文正确地将 binary F1 (gen-1 vs gen-2+) 作为 primary metric，但这实质上将核心任务降级为二分类。Confusion matrix（Appendix G）显示 gen-0 recall = 99.4%，几乎完全由 seed overlap 驱动。Independent-seed 实验（Appendix T）仅使用 2 个 generator（Pythia-2.8B, OLMo），不足以验证 full 3-class LOGO 的可靠性。\n- 为什么影响接收：论文的 Contribution 1 宣称 \"formulate generation depth classification...gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+\"，但实际上只能可靠评估 gen-1 vs gen-2+ 的二元区分。3-class F1 = 0.762 被标注为 \"may carry upward bias\"（Table 1 脚注），但仍在 Abstract 中作为结果呈现。这使得论文的核心 framing（三分类 depth profiling）与实际实验支撑之间存在显著落差。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 在 full 8-generator LOGO 设置下进行 independent-seed 实验，为每个 generator 使用不同的 seed pool，报告干净的 3-class LOGO F1；(2) 或者将论文 framing 调整为 \"depth profiling 以 binary gen-1 vs gen-2+ 区分为核心\"，降低 3-class 叙事的权重；(3) Abstract 应明确说明 3-class metric 受 seed sharing 影响，binary F1 是唯一 leak-free 指标。\n\n**N2** [critical] 仅限 base model 的限制对论文 significance 造成根本性约束\n- 具体位置：§5 Discussion, §Limitations, Appendix K (instruct)\n- 问题：论文明确限定为 base (pretrained) models，但 web 上绝大多数 LLM 生成文本来自 instruction-tuned / RLHF-aligned 模型。Appendix K 显示 instruct 模型下 entropy mean ρ 翻转符号（−0.60 → +0.48），6/7 特征方向反转（OLMo, Qwen），LOGO F1 从 0.763 暴跌至 0.338（OLMo-Instruct）。这不是一个需要微调的边缘问题——DDR 的核心结构（哪些特征 drift、哪些 stationary）在 instruct model 下被根本性地颠覆。\n- 为什么影响接收：如果 DDR 是 base model 独有的现象，其科学价值仍然存在（理解 recursive generation dynamics），但论文多处暗示更广泛的适用性（Introduction: \"how language models transform distributions under iterative conditioning\"; Related Work 中与 model collapse 的联系），这些 claim 的范围超出了实验支撑。当前 framing 让读者可能高估方法的实际部署价值。\n- 建议修改方向：(1) 在 Introduction 和 Abstract 中更显著地标注 base-model-only 限制（不仅在最后一句提及）；(2) 将 instruct model 结果从附录提升到正文 Discussion 中作为核心发现；(3) 讨论 DDR 特征方向反转的理论含义——这是否意味着 instruct model 的 recursive generation 具有质性不同的 dynamics？\n\n---\n\n## Should-Fix 问题（影响评分但不一票否决）\n\n**W1** [warning] Tail decay 作为 \"near-stationary\" 特征的分类缺乏充分统计支撑\n- 具体位置：§4.2, Table 5 (Appendix R), Table 6 (Appendix S)\n- 问题：TOST 等效性检验（ε = 0.20）中，tail decay 仅 4/8 generators 通过，是 near-stationary 组中通过率最低的。Non-passing generators 的 |d| 范围为 0.15–0.22（Table 5），其中 OLMo 的 d=0.22 已超过 TOST bound。Sensitivity analysis 显示需要放松至 ε=0.25 才能达到 7/8 通过（Table 6）。将一个仅半数通过等效性检验的特征归入 \"near-stationary\" 组，削弱了 DDR 3/1/3 partition 的说服力。\n- 建议：(1) 考虑将 tail decay 移至 intermediate 组或标记为 \"weakly stationary\"；(2) 或在正文中充分讨论其边界地位，而非仅在附录中处理。\n\n**W2** [warning] Downstream utility 实验统计效力极低（n=3 seeds），不足以支撑 \"null result\" claim\n- 具体位置：Appendix M, §5 Discussion\n- 问题：Downstream fine-tuning 实验仅用 3 random seeds。Cohen's d 值高达 86.0 和 119.7（论文正确指出这是 artificial inflation），但 TOST 结果的可推广性同样有限。论文承认 \"the TOST results have limited generalizability\"，但 Discussion 仍将此作为关键结论之一引用：\"Depth composition does not determine downstream training utility at fixed sample count\"（§4.3, §5）。以 n=3 的实验支撑一个跨 Section 反复引用的结论，统计基础过于薄弱。\n- 建议：增加 seeds 至至少 10–20，或将该结论降级为 \"preliminary observation\"，在正文引用时加上 qualification。\n\n**W3** [warning] 4-detector ensemble 的 baseline 结果反过来削弱了 DDR framework 的方法论贡献\n- 具体位置：Table 2 (§4.3)\n- 问题：4-detector ensemble binary F1 = 0.708 > 7-feature ensemble 0.705，4-det. + surface = 0.708。虽然论文将此定位为 \"contextualizing depth discrimination ability rather than claim competitive advantage\"，但这个结果意味着：(1) 现有检测方法已隐含深度信息；(2) 简单组合现有检测器就能达到或超过 DDR-based 方法。论文试图用 \"inference cost\" 差异（2.4×）来区分，但成本差距并不显著。DDR 的真正价值应在于其 interpretability 和分析框架，但论文对此的阐述不够充分。\n- 建议：重新组织 baseline 讨论，重点强调 DDR 的分析价值（为什么 tri-partition 重要）而非分类性能竞争力。\n\n**W4** [warning] 多个 generator subset 混用增加了 cross-experiment 比较的复杂性\n- 具体位置：Appendix V (Table 9), 全文各处\n- 问题：论文在不同实验中使用 5/6/8 generator 子集（cross-model chains: 5-gen, temperature: 4-gen, prompt-type: 1-gen, QLoRA: 6-gen, main LOGO: 8-gen）。虽然 Appendix V 记录了这些差异并解释为 \"chronological expansion\"，但导致以下问题：(1) Cross-reference 验证（Table 4, 5-gen LOGO）的结果无法直接与 main results（8-gen LOGO）对比；(2) Prompt-type 实验仅用 Pythia-2.8B 一个 generator，cross-prompt generalization 的 claim 缺乏跨模型验证；(3) 审稿人难以判断哪些结论仅基于子集有效。\n- 建议：(1) 在核心 claim 上统一使用 8-gen LOGO 评估；(2) 对 prompt-type 实验增加至少 2–3 个 generators。\n\n**W5** [warning] Cross-domain 实验使用 t=1.0（超出可靠范围），domain 和 temperature 效应混淆\n- 具体位置：Appendix I\n- 问题：Cross-domain 实验使用 t=1.0, top-p=1.0（论文自身在 Appendix H 中确认 t≥0.9 时方法退化 9.3–26.0 pp），导致 domain generalization 与 temperature sensitivity 无法分离。论文诚实地标注了这一 confound，并补充了 t=0.7 结果，但 t=0.7 下 Academic domain binary F1 降至 0.628，反而暗示了 cross-domain 信号可能比 t=1.0 结果所示的更弱。\n- 建议：在 Limitations 中更显著地讨论这一混淆。Cross-domain claim 应基于 t=0.7 结果而非 t=1.0。\n\n**W6** [warning] Cohen's d 作为 DDR 分区主要指标存在异方差问题\n- 具体位置：§4.2 \"Distributional shift quantification\"\n- 问题：Levene tests 拒绝了 drifting features 的等方差假设（p < 10^−10，方差比 1.86–2.21），但正文在定义 DDR partition 的关键阈值（d > 0.75 vs d < 0.10）时仍然使用 pooled Cohen's d。Heteroscedasticity-robust 指标（Glass's Δ, Cliff's δ）仅在 Appendix S 中报告。虽然 robust 指标确认了 partition（gap ≥ 9.5×），但主要叙事完全依赖的是不满足假设条件的 pooled d。\n- 建议：在正文 §4.2 中同时报告 Glass's Δ 或 Cliff's δ，或至少注明 pooled d 在异方差条件下为保守估计。\n\n---\n\n## 小问题（不影响接收）\n\n- **M1**: Abstract 中 \"base (pretrained)\" 出现 2 次，§3.1–3.2 中再次出现，重复注释显得冗余。保留首次括号解释即可。\n- **M2**: Table 1 脚注 \"†3-class F1 may carry upward bias from gen-0 seed sharing\" 是关键信息，但脚注过于不显眼。应在 caption 主体中说明。\n- **M3**: Feature correlation matrix（Appendix P, Table 7）显示 entropy mean 与 TTR/hapax 的 Pearson r = 0.86/0.81。这意味着 3 个 \"drifting\" features 共享大量方差，DDR 中的 \"三个独立 drifting 信号\" 实际上可能是同一个 latent factor 的不同投影。论文在 §4.2 提到 TTR-hapax 相关性但未充分讨论 entropy mean 与二者的高相关性对 DDR 解释的影响。\n- **M4**: Qualitative example（Appendix X, Table 10）中 gen-2 文本明显退化为简单重复（\"the son of the God of the Sky and the Goddess of the Earth\" 重复 4 次），这种极端退化在较大模型中可能不常见。建议补充非 Pythia 模型的定性示例。\n- **M5**: Ethics Statement 中 \"62 GPU-hours on 4× RTX 5090\" 合理，但缺少 CO₂ 排放估算（ARR 鼓励但不强制）。\n- **M6**: 正文第 8 页 Discussion 中 \"gen-1 per-class F1 = 0.63\" 引用了 Appendix L 的结果，但 cross-model per-class 表（Appendix N, Table 8）中 gen-1 F1 = 0.768。应明确说明 0.63 指的是哪个评估设置。\n\n---\n\n## Questions（需要作者回答）\n\n**Q1**: Independent-seed 实验（Appendix T）仅用 2 个 generators。能否在 full 8-generator LOGO 设置下报告 independent-seed 3-class F1？这是验证 gen-0 分类是否真正基于 distributional properties（而非 seed identity）的关键实验。\n\n**Q2**: Cross-reference 实验（Table 4）使用 5-gen LOGO。在 full 8-gen 设置下（加入 Llama, Mistral），cross-reference spread 是否仍 ≤ 0.013？考虑到 Mistral-7B 的 binary F1 最低（0.633），reference model 选择可能对困难 generators 更敏感。\n\n**Q3**: DDR tri-partition 中 3 个 drifting features 高度相关（entropy mean–TTR: r=0.86, entropy mean–hapax: r=0.81）。是否考虑过用 PCA 或 factor analysis 验证 drifting group 是否实际上对应单一 latent factor？如果是，DDR 的 \"三分\" 可能更准确地描述为 \"一个 drifting 因子 + 1 intermediate + 3 stationary\"。\n\n**Q4**: 论文使用固定 prompt \"Continue the following text: {text}\"。在 real-world 场景中，递归生成链中的 prompt 可能不同（不同用户使用不同 prompt 风格）。Cross-prompt LOPTO 实验仅用 1 个 generator（Pythia-2.8B）。在多 generator 设置下，cross-prompt transfer 是否同样稳健？\n\n**Q5**: Appendix H 显示 Qwen3-1.7B 在 t=0.5 和 t=0.7 下的 binary F1 完全相同（0.751），95% CI 缺失（\"---\"）。这是数据问题还是数值巧合？CI 缺失的原因是什么？\n\n**Q6**: Table 2 中 \"7-feature (ours)\" 的 F1 (0.774/0.705) 与 Table 1 中 8-gen LOGO average (0.762/0.694) 存在差异。Table 2 caption 说明是 \"pooled evaluation\"。Pooled vs per-generator macro average 的差异从何而来？是否因为 pooled evaluation 在 class distribution 不同的 generator 上加权不同？\n\n---\n\n## Missing References\n\n- 论文引用覆盖度总体充足（68 条引用），related work 的组织合理。以下为可补充方向（非必须）：\n  - **Bao et al. (2024)** \"On the Reliability of AI Text Detectors\" — 关于检测器可靠性的分析，与 baseline comparison 相关\n  - 更多关于 ordinal regression 在 NLP 中的应用文献（当前主要引用通用 ML 领域的 ordinal methods）\n  - Guo et al. (2023) \"How Close is ChatGPT to Human Experts?\" — 关于 LLM 生成文本特性的早期分析\n\n---\n\n## 主要优势\n\n1. **任务定义新颖且有意义**: Generation depth classification 将 binary detection 推进到更细粒度的 provenance 分析，是一个自然且重要的 research direction。与 model collapse 文献的联系建立得很好。\n\n2. **实验控制极为严谨**: LOGO + LOFO + seed-level demeaning + independent-seed + length-matched + random-subsample control 构成了多层次的 confound control pipeline，体现了对实验设计的深入思考。很少见到如此完整的 control 链。\n\n3. **DDR tri-partition 的分析框架有价值**: 即使分类性能一般，将特征空间分解为 drifting/intermediate/near-stationary 提供了一个理解 recursive generation dynamics 的有用透镜。多指标交叉验证（ρ, d, KS, Levene, TOST, Glass's Δ, Cliff's δ, clustering）和 holdout split validation 增强了可信度。\n\n4. **诚实的 limitation 讨论**: 论文对 base-model 限制、instruction tuning 退化、temperature sensitivity、3-class metric inflation 等问题的自我批评到位，没有 overclaim。这种透明性值得肯定。\n\n5. **QLoRA upper bound 实验**: 3-class F1 = 0.905（vs 0.789 XGBoost）证实深度信号在 raw token embeddings 中确实存在，同时也说明 7-feature set 是有损压缩。这个对比有助于定位 handcrafted features 的价值。\n\n6. **完整的 ablation 覆盖**: LOFO, feature group ablation, classifier comparison, finer-grained depth, cross-temperature, cross-domain, human editing robustness, error analysis — 附录全面且有组织。\n\n---\n\n## 信心说明\n\nConfidence: 4/5。我仔细审阅了全部正文（8页）和全部 20+ 个附录，对论文涉及的 AI-generated text detection、effect size 统计方法、ordinal classification 均有领域经验。对 tokenizer-level 的 NLL 计算细节关注较少（confidence 扣分点）。\n",
```

以下是 Reviewer 2 的完整审稿意见，请逐条回应：对每个 Weakness/Question 标注 [AGREE] 同意并说明理由，或 [REBUT] 反驳并提供证据（cite 具体 Section/Table/Figure）。

# Reviewer 2 审稿报告

## Summary
本文提出"生成深度分类"(generation depth classification)这一新任务，将文本按递归生成深度(gen-0/gen-1/gen-2+)进行分类，而非传统的二元真/合成检测。作者设计了7个手工特征并提出 DDR (Differential Depth Response) 三分法框架，在8个 base 模型上用 LOGO 交叉验证进行评估，binary F1=0.694、3-class F1=0.694。实验充分但方法论新颖性有限（特征工程+XGBoost），且实际应用价值尚未得到充分论证——论文自身实验显示 depth-aware curation 对下游 perplexity 无显著改善。

## Must-Fix

**N1 [critical] 实际应用动机薄弱——下游效用实验自我否定核心价值。**
- 论文 Introduction（§1）将 depth classification 定位为理解 model collapse 和数据溯源的工具。但 Appendix L 的 TOST 等价检验表明 depth-aware curation 与 random subsampling 在下游 perplexity 上"practically indistinguishable"。这意味着知道文本的生成深度对数据筛选没有实际帮助。如果深度分类对下游任务无可衡量的价值，论文的 motivation 从 "this is useful" 退化为 "this is possible but we don't know why it matters"。

**N2 [critical] 仅限 base models，严重限制实际适用性。**
- 所有实验仅在 pretrained（base）模型上进行。现实中绝大多数 LLM 生成文本来自 instruction-tuned 或 RLHF 模型。§4.2 和 Contribution 4 提到 "instruction tuning reverses feature drift direction"，但这是从 OLMo-Instruct 一个模型的简单观察，而非系统实验。

**N3 [critical] DDR "框架"的理论贡献被过度包装。**
- DDR 本质上是将7个特征按其与 depth 的 Spearman 相关系数分为三组。这是描述性分析（descriptive categorization），不是一个"框架"。将相关性分组冠以 "Differential Depth Response" 的名称并作为核心贡献之一，有过度包装之嫌。

## Should-Fix

**W1 [warning] 参考模型依赖（Pythia-410M）是未充分讨论的系统性混淆。**
- 所有 reference-model features 依赖于 Pythia-410M。当生成器与参考模型属于同一家族时，depth signal 可能部分来自模型家族相似性。Table 1 显示 Pythia-2.8B 的 binary F1 最高（0.738）。

**W2 [warning] 共享 seed passage 引入的统计依赖未充分处理。**
- 所有8个生成器共享同一组 ~1,000 个 Wikipedia seed passages，违反 independence 假设。Appendix R 对照只用了6个生成器的 subset。

**W3 [warning] 3-class 分组（gen-0 / gen-1 / gen-2+）的决策缺乏充分论证。**
- §3.1 将 depth 2-4 合并为 "gen-2+"，但没有清楚解释为什么不选择 4-class 或其他粒度。

**W4 [warning] Baseline 对比不完整。**
- 缺少 n-gram 统计特征 baseline、预训练 encoder classifier baseline（如 RoBERTa finetune）、直接使用 perplexity 作为 depth classifier 的 baseline。

## Minor
- M1: Introduction 的 model collapse 和 data provenance 双动机在 Discussion 中未均等发展
- M2: Table 1 caption 与 Appendix R 对 seed sharing 的措辞不一致
- M3: Figure 1 x轴标签字体偏小
- M4: §2.3 引用密度偏低
- M5: Appendix T 仅1条链示例

## Questions
- Q1: 如果 depth classification 对数据筛选无帮助，最有价值的应用场景是什么？
- Q2: DDR 三分法阈值（|ρ|=0.60, 0.10）如何确定？sensitivity analysis？
- Q3: 为什么不讨论 iterative self-training / self-play 文献？
- Q4: Pythia 家族生成器是否因架构相似性获得系统性优势？
- Q5: instruction tuning "reverses feature drift direction" 基于多少个模型？

## Missing References
- Self-training/self-play: Yuan et al. 2024 "Self-Rewarding LMs"; Chen et al. 2024 "SPIN"
- Data attribution: Park et al. 2023 "TRAK"
- Lexical diversity: MTLD (McCarthy & Jarvis 2010), MATTR (Covington & McFall 2010)

## Overall: 3.0 (Findings), Confidence: 4/5

```text
The Review Director is asking me (Reviewer 1) to respond to Reviewer 2's complete review, going through each weakness and question and marking [AGREE] or [REBUT] with evidence from the paper.

Let me go through each point carefully.

**N1**: Downstream utility experiment self-negates core value.
- I partially agree but need to nuance. The paper doesn't claim depth classification is useful for data curation - it explicitly frames this as a "diagnostic rather than prescriptive role" (§5). The motivation is understanding recursive generation dynamics and data provenance, not filtering. However, R2 has a point that the motivation could be stronger.


**N2**: Base models only.
- I raised this same issue as my N2. I agree.

**N3**: DDR "framework" is over-packaged.
- I see R2's concern about the terminology, though DDR does integrate multiple convergent metrics (ρ, d, KS, TOST, Glass's Δ, Cliff's δ, clustering) with holdout validation and heteroscedasticity analysis. The label "framework" might be generous for what's fundamentally a descriptive partitioning approach.

**W1**: Reference model dependency (Pythia-410M).
- The paper addresses this through cross-reference experiments in Table 4, though the point about limited model diversity still stands.

**W2**: Shared seed passages.
- I had similar concerns about this. The paper includes seed-level demeaning (F1=0.701) and an independent-seed control (F1=0.680) in the appendix, so they do attempt to isolate this effect.

**W3**: 3-class grouping.
- Looking at the justification in Appendix D for why three classes were chosen.

**W4**: Baseline comparison incomplete.
- The paper includes Avg-LogProb as a perplexity baseline and QLoRA fine-tuning as an upper bound, but it's missing n-gram baselines and RoBERTa comparisons that R2 requested.

**Questions**:
- Q1: Application scenario - positioned as diagnostic/provenance
- Q2: DDR thresholds - sensitivity analysis is in Appendix S
- Q3: Self-training literature - reasonable suggestion
- Q4: Pythia family advantage - cross-reference experiments address this
- Q5: Instruction tuning reversal - observed across OLMo, Qwen, and Llama

Now I'll draft my responses.
```

的 1 条链示例，补充其他模型的定性示例是合理建议。\n\n---\n\n## Questions\n\n**R2-Q1**: \"如果 depth classification 对数据筛选无帮助，最有价值的应用场景是什么？\"\n→ 合理问题。论文 §5 提到 \"corpus-level flagging\"（gen-1 per-class F1=0.63）和 model collapse dynamics 理解。但我认为作者需要更具体地阐述：DDR 的 location/shape decomposition 对 model collapse 研究的具体启示是什么？\n\n**R2-Q2**: \"DDR 三分法阈值如何确定？\"\n→ 论文实际上有 sensitivity analysis。§4.2 写道 \"shifting thresholds by ±0.10 does not alter grouping\"。Appendix S 的 TOST bound sensitivity（Table 6）和 holdout split validation（Table 7）进一步确认 partition 对阈值选择的稳定性。R2 可能遗漏了这些分析。\n\n**R2-Q3**: \"为什么不讨论 iterative self-training / self-play 文献？\"\n→ 合理建议。Self-training/self-play 也涉及 iterative generation，但属于 training-loop setting（模型权重更新），与论文的 inference-chain setting（固定模型参数）有本质区别。§2.2 第一段已明确区分 \"training-loop drift\" 和 \"inference-chain drift\"。但简要提及 self-training 文献作为对比仍有价值。\n\n**R2-Q4**: \"Pythia 家族是否因架构相似性获得系统性优势？\"\n→ 见 W1 回应。Cross-reference 实验（Table 4）已回答：换用 OLMo 或 Qwen 作为 reference，spread ≤ 0.013。\n\n**R2-Q5**: \"instruction tuning reversal 基于多少个模型？\"\n→ R2 称 \"一个模型\"，实际是 3 个模型家族（OLMo, Qwen, Llama），见 Appendix K Table 3。R2 的事实错误。\n\n---\n\n## Missing References\n\n**[AGREE]** Self-training 文献（Yuan et al. 2024, Chen et al. 2024 SPIN）可在 §2.2 简要提及作为 training-loop iterative generation 的一种。\n\n**[REBUT]** TRAK (Park et al. 2023) 是 data attribution（追踪训练样本对预测的影响），与 generation depth provenance 是完全不同的问题。引用价值有限。\n\n**[REBUT]** MTLD 和 MATTR 实际上已被引用。§2.3 原文：\"more sophisticated alternatives exist (MTLD, HD-D (McCarthy and Jarvis, 2010); MATTR (Covington and McFall, 2010))\"。R2 遗漏了论文中已有的引用。\n\n---\n\n## 总结\n\nR2 的 N1 和 N3 存在对论文 framing 的误读：论文明确将 DDR 定位为 diagnostic framework 而非 engineering tool，下游 null result 是 supporting evidence 而非 self-negation；DDR 的建立经过多层验证，不是简单的相关系数分组。N2（base-model 限制）我完全同意且在自己的审稿中同样标记为 critical。W3（3-class 分组）已有 5-class 实验验证。W1（reference model）已有 cross-reference 实验回答。W4（baseline 不完整）部分同意（缺 n-gram baseline），但 R2 遗漏了论文已有的 Avg-LogProb 和 QLoRA 实验。R2 对 instruct model 实验数量和 MTLD/MATTR 引用的事实判断有误。\n",

```json
  "project": "gen-depth-fingerprint"
}
```
